PREFACE, 



The following woik is inteaded ior the instrao" 
lioft of giHend readers, to direct their attentiaii to 
the study of the hemTens^ and to prssent to their 
view suMime objects of eontenqplatioiL With this 
Tiew the author has avoided entering on the more 
atetnise and recondite peitions q£ astronomical 
seieoee, and cenfiaed his attention chiefly to the 
ediUiitlon oH fauMs^ the Ibandatien on which they* 
rest, and the reasoniage hy which Aey are sup* 
pertsd. HI the prominent fade and discof eries 
6»uiected with deacripit? e astsonony, in so far ao 
they relate to the planetiuy ayslem, are here re-^ 
c<Nrded, and many of diem exhibited*in a new pmnt 
of Hew; and several new facts and observations 
are detailed nHiidi have hitherto been either lumo- 
Qced or tmreeofued* 

The residts of hm&eds Of tedious calovlations 
have been Introduced respeckiiiff die solid and su* 
perficia! contents of ^ diftMH planets, their eat« 
eBites, and the riilp of tSatcmi \ their comparativo 
magnitudes and motions, the ezieni of their orbits^' 



IT PREFACE. 

the apparent magnitudes of bodies in their respect- 
ive firmaments, and manj other particulars not 
contained in books of astronomy, in order to pro- 
duce in the minds of common readers definite con- 

• 

ceptions of the magnitude and grandeur of the so- 
lar system. The mode of deteimining the distan- 
ces and magnitudes of the celestial bodies is ex- 
platnedt and renderied as perspicuous and popular 
as the . nature of the subject will admit ; and the 
prominent arguments which demonstrate a plural . 
ity of worlds: are considered in all their bearings, 
and illustrated in detail. 

One new department of astronomical science^ 
which has hitherto been overlooked, has been in-: 
troduced into this volume, namely f. the, scenery ofs 
the heavens <is exhibited from the surfaces of the: 
different planets, and their ^o^eZ/iW, which. forms- 
an interesting object of contemplation,- and, at ahe. 
same, time, a presumptive argument ia favour of the 
doctrine of a piturality of worlds. 

The author, having for many years past been a. 
pretty constant observer of celestial phenomena, 
was under no necessity of adhering implicitly to 
the descriptions given by preceding writers, hav- 
ing had an opportunity of observing, through some 
of the best. reflecting and achromatic telescopes, 
the greater part of the phenomena of the solar sya- 
tem which are h^re described. 



pftBFAcc. r 

Througboat the Volunie he has endeavoured to 
make the facts he describes bear upon the illus- 
tration of the power, Wiadom, Benevolence, and 
the Moral Government of the Almighty, and to 
elevate the views of the reader to the contempla^ 
tion of Him who sits on the throne of the universe, 
" by whom the worlds were framed,'* and who is 
the Source and Centre of all felicity. 

In prosecuting the subject of Celestial Scenery, 
the author intends, in another volume, to carry 
forward his survey to the Starrf Heavens and 
other objects connected with astronomy. That 
volume will embrace discussions relative to the 
number, distance, and arrangement of the stars ; 
periodical and variable stars ; new and temporary 
stars; double and triple stars; binary systems; 
stellar and planetary nebulae; the comets, and 
other particulars ; accompanied with such reflec- 
tions as the contemplation of such august objects 
may suggest. The subject of a plurality of worlds 
will likewise be prosecuted, and additional argu* 
ments, derived both from reason and Revelation, 
will be adduced in support of this position. The 
practical utility of astronomical studies, their con- 
nexion with religion, and the views they unfold of 
the perfections and the empire of the Creator, wiU 
also be the subject of consideration. And should 

the limits of a single volume permit, some hints 

A2 



may be giTen in relatioa to ^bm dmderaim in as- 
tnMiomy:, and the means fay which the progreee of 
the science may be promoted, together with de» 
scriptions of the telescope, die equatorial, and other 
instnmients, and the manner of using them for 
lestial investigation. '

Here, then, we have presented to view a scene the most

nme time, from east to west. This we find to be the cast 
witk Jupiter and Saturn, which are a thousand times larger

when we are sailing down its current in a steamboat towards 
the east. 

2. Because ii is impossible to concevie that so maiiy bodies 
of different magnitudes, and at diifferent distances from the 
oarthj could all have the same period cf diurnal revolviion. 
The sun is four hundred times farther from us than the moon« 
lad is sixty millions of times larger. Saturn and Herschel 
■re still farther from the earth ; the comets are of different 
sizes, and traverse the heavens in all directions and at differ* 
ent distances ; the fixed stars are evidently placed at differ- 
ent distances from the earth and from each other ; yet all 
tiiese bodies have exactly the same period of revolution, even 
to a single moment, if the heavens revolve around the earth, 
and that, toOj notwithstanding the other motions, in various 
directions, which many of them perform. It is, therefore, 
much more natural and reasonable to suppose that the earth 
revolves around its axis, since this circumstance solves all the 
phenomena and removes every difficulty. 

3. Because snck a rate of motion in the heavenly bodies, if 
it could be supposed to exist, would soon shatter thsm to 
atoms. Were a ball of wood to be projected from a camion 
at the rate of a thousand miles an hour» in a few moments it 
would be reduced to splinters ; and hence the forage and other 
soft substances projected from a musket or a piece of onl« 
nance are instantly torn to pieces. What, then, might be 
supposed to be the consequence, were a body impelled 
through the regions of space with a velocity of a hundred and 
forty thousand millions of miles in a moment of time 1 It 
would most assuredly reduce to atoms the most compact bod-

The above are a few azupments which, when p ro pe r ly 
weighed, ought to carry conviction to the mind of eveiy r^ 
tkmal inquirer, that the general motion which appean in the 
stsny heavens is not real, but is caused by the rotation of the 
earth round its axis every day, by which we and all the inhab* 
itants of the globe are carried round in a regular and uniform 
motion from west to east. When this conclusion is admitted, 
it removes every difficulty and every disproportion which at 
first sppeared in the motions and arrangements of the celestial 
orbs, and reduces the system of the umverse to a scene of 
beauty, harmony, and order worthy of the infinite vnsdom of. 
Him who formed the plan of the mighty fabiic, and who aeU 
tied ^ the ordinances of heaven." Instead, then, of ramain* 
ing in a state of absolute rest, as we are at first apt to ima- 
gine, we are transported every moment towards the east with 
a motion ten times more rapid than has ever been effiscted by 
steam-eairiages or air-balloons. It is troe, we do not feel this

In October this constellation will appear nearly in the poaition 
represented in fig. 1 (p. 24) ; in April^it will appear nearly as 
represented in fig. 3 (p. 27). These variations in the appear- 
ance of the stars lead us to conclude that there is an apparent 
annual motion in these luminaries. This motion may be oh* 
served, if we take notice, for a few days or weeks, of those 
stars which are situated near the path of the sun. When we 
see a bright star near the western horizon, a little elevated 
above the place where the sun went down, if we continue oar 
observation we shall find that every day it appears less ele- 
vated at the same hour, and seems to be gradually approach^' 
inff to the point of the heavens in which the sun is situated, 
tiU, in the course of a week or tWo, it ceases to be visible, 
being overpowered by the superior brightness of the sun. In 
the course of a month or two the same star which disappeared 
in the west wiU be seen rising some time before the sun m 
the east, bavins passed from the eastern side of the sun to a 
distance considerably westward of him. The stars in the 
western quarter of the heavens which appeared more elevated 
will be found gradually to approximate to the sun, tiU they 
likewise disappear ; and in this manner all the stars of heaven 
seem to have a revolution, distinct from their diurnal, from 
east to west, which is accompliahed in the course of a year. 

The different positions of the PlaadeSt or seven stars, at 
different seasona of the year, will afford every observer an op- 
portunity of perceiving this motion. About the middle of 
September these stars will be seen, about eight o^dock in the 
evening, a little to the south of the northeaat point of the 
horizon ; about the middle of January, at the same hotur, they 
will be seen on the meridian, or due south ; on the first of 
March they will be seen half way between the zenith and the 
western horizon ; about the middle of April they will appear 
very near the horizon ; soon after which they will be over- 
powered by the solar ravs, and will remain invisible for neariy 
two months, after which thejr will reappear in the east, eariy 
in the morninff, before the rising sun. 

This annual motion of the stars evidently indicates that th« 
son has an apparent motion every day from vfeat to eaet, con-

west. This apptrent motion is at the rate of nearly a degree 
every day, a space nearly equal to twice the sun's sf^arent

around the whole heav.ens, from west to east, in the coarse of 
a year. This apparent motion of the sun is caused by the 
annual revolution of the earth around the sun as the centre 
of its motion, which completely accounts for all the apparent 
movements in the sun and stars to which we have now ad- 
verted. If we place a candle upon a table in the midst of a 
room, and walk round it in a circle, and, as we proceed, mark 
the different parts of the opposite walls with which the candle 
appears coincident, when we have con^)leted our 'circle the 
candle will appear to have made a revolution round the room. 
If the walls be conceived to represent the starry heavens, and 
the candle the sun, it will convey a rude idea of the apparent mo- 
tion of the sun, and the different clusters of stars which ap- 
pear at different seasons of the year in consequence of the 
annual motion of the earth. But this subject will be more 
particularly explained in the sequel. 

From what we have now stated iu relation to the apparent 
motions of the heavens, we are necessarily led to conceive of 
the earth as a body, placed, as it were, in the midst of infinite 
space, and surrounded in every direction, above, below, on the 
right hand and on the left» with the luminaries of heaven, which 
display their radiance from every quarter at immeasurable 
distances;. and that its annual and diurnal motions account 
for all the movements which appear in the celestial sphere. 
Hence it is a necessary conclusion, that we are surrounded at 
all times with a host of" stars, in the da'^time as well as in the 
night, although they are then imperceptible. The reason why 
they are invisible during the day is obviously that their fainter 
light is overpowered by the more vivid splendour of the sun 
and the reflective power of the atmosphere. But although 
they are then imperceptible to the unassisted eye, they can be 
distinctly perceived,' not only in the mornings and evenings,^ 
but even at noonday, while the. sun is sUning bright, by 
means of telescopes adapted to an equatorial motion ; and in 
this way almost every star visible to the naked ^e at nig^t 
ean be pointed out, even amid the effulgence of day, when 
it is within the boundary of our hemisphere. When the stars

any-other number, had mathematicians chosen, might have

It may not be improper to i^emark, that when we state the 
number of degrees between two objects, either on the eurth. 
01 in the heavens, it is not intended to eipress the teal die* 
tance, but only the relative or appcareni distance of the objects. 
Thus, when we say that two places On the earth, which he di- 
rectly north and south of each other, are twenty degrees di»* 
tant, it does not convey an idea of the aciuAl distance of these 
places from each other, but ovlj what proportion of the earth*e 
circumference intervenes between them. If, however, we 
know the number of yards or miles contained in that circum- 
ference, or in a single degree of it, we can then find the actual 
distance, by multipl3rinffthe number of degrees by the numbet 
of miles in a degree. But this supposes that tfa^ extent of a 
degree on the earth's surface has been measured, and the 
number of yards or miles it contains ascertained. In like 
manner, when we say that two stars in the helivens ate fifteen 
degrees from each other, this merely expresses their relative 
position, or what portion of a great circle of the celestii^ 
sphere intervenes between them, but determines, nothing as te 
their real distance^ which is fat surpassing our comprehend 
sion. The real magnitude of objects or spaces in the neavens 
depends upon then: distance. Thus, the apparent breadth or 
diameter of the moon is about half a degree, or nearly thirty- 
two minutes, and that of the sun nearly the same ; but as thf 
moon is much nearer to us than the sun, a nUniUe of a degree 
on her surface is equal only to about seventy miles, while ^ 
minute on the stm's surface is 6qual to more than 28,000 
ksile^ which is four hund^red times greater. The greyest

ncondi, or nearly a minate ; but as Saturn is mQck fiurthw 
firom us than Venus, his real diameter is 79,000 miles, while 
that of Venus is only 7,700. Be/ore the rea) diameter of any 
cibject in the heayens can be determined, its distance must be 
first ascertained.

It would be no easy task to describe how their epicycles 
could be made to move through the thick crusts of crystal ol 
which their spheres were mad^. They, however, found some 
means or other to extricate themselves from eveiy difficulty; 
as they always had recourse to geometrical linea, which never 
found any obstacle to their passage on paper. To make all 
the pieces of their machinery move with as much smoothness 
and as little inconsistency as possible, ther were forced* to 
delineate certain furrows, or to notch on the arches certain 
grooves, in which they jointed and made the tenons and 
mortises of their epicycles to slide. All this celestial joiner's 
work, to which succeeding astronomers added several pieces 
to produce balancings, or perpetual goings backward and 
forward, had no other tendency than to conceal the sublime 
and beautiful simplicity of nature, and to prevent mankind, 
for manv aees, from recognising the true system of the world.

Without admitting the motion of the earth, it would surpass

sufficient to establish the opimon of the earth being a quiescent 
body, it was generally received by the learned in Europe till 
the sixteenth century, or a little after the period of the Kefor»

each a clumsy and bungling system was so long in vogue, is

to submit to the authority of Aristotle, than to follow the path

From this brief sketch of the Ptolemaic system, we may 
learn into how many absurdities we mvolve ourselves by the

single false principle ; and the importance of substantiating 
every fiict and proving every principle in all our investiga- 
tions of the system of nature and the order of the universe.

In this system the sun is considered as placed near the 
centre. Around this central luminary the planets perform 
their revolutions in the following order : — ^First, the planet 
Mercury, at the distance from the sun's centre of about 87 
millions of miles. Next to Mercury is Venus, distinguished 
by the name of the morning and evening star, at the £stance 
of 31 millions of miles from the orbit of Mercury, and 68 
millions from the sun. The Earth is considered as the planet 
next in order, which revolves at the distance of 96 milliona c^ 
miles from the sun, and 27 millions from the orbit of Venus. 
Farther from the sun than the Earth is the planet Mars, 
which is 145 millions of miles fr<mi the sun, and 50 millions 
beyond the orbit of the Earth. Next to the orbit of Mars 
are four small planetary bodies, sometimes named Aateroidt, 
which were discovered at different times about the beginning 
of the present century. They are named Vesta, Juno, Geres, 
and PaUas. Of these, the first in order from the sun is Vesta, 
St the distance of 225 millions of miles ; the next, Juno, at 
the distance of 253 millions Ceres, at 260 millions ; and 
Pallas, at 266 millions of miles. The planet Jupiter is the 
next in order, and performs its revolution in an orbit 495 
millions of miles from the sun, and 400 from the orbit of the 
earth. Saturn is nearly double the distance of Jupiter from 
the sun, being distant from that orb above 900 millions of 
miles. The most distant planet in the system which has yet 
been discovered is Uranus, or Herschel, which is removed 
from the sun at more than double the distance of Saturn ; 
namely, above 1800 millions of miles. The orbit of this

t 4. In the airangements of the planets in the system, at

"When Copernicus first proposed his system, it was one of the

iscovered ; and little more than Iwlf a century intervened,

^temicus), in the hands of Galileo, determined to a certainty

eminent astronomer. How great, may we suppose, would

Venus, when she appears most brilhant, exhibits, in reality,

the truth of his system has now become one! of the strongest 
and most palpable demonstrations of thie reality of that ar- 
rangement which haa placed the sun ii^ the centre, and set 
the earth in motion between Mars and Venus.

Tins planet is the nearest to the sun of any that hate yet

sun than they can to us. This planet, on account of its 
moving in the neighbourhood of the sun, is seldom noticed by 
a'common observer. It is only to be seen by the naked eye 
about the period of its greatest elongation irom the sun, which 
is sometimes only about 16^ or 17^, and never exceeds 29^.' 
These elongations happen, at an averaae, aboat six or seren 
times every year ; about three times when the planet is east- 
ward of the sun, and three times when it is to the westward. 
This planet, therefore, can olJy be seen by the unassisted eye 
te a few days about these periods, either in the morning b 
tiMe before snuise, or in the evenings, a Uttle after sunset.

The light or the intensity' of solar radiation which falls oi 
this planet is nearly seyeii times greater than that which faC 
upon the earth ; for the proportion of their distances from tl| 
sun is nearly as three to eight, and the quantity of li^ht di. 
fused from a luminous body is as the square of the distance 
from that body. The square of 3 is 9, and the square of< 
8, 64, which, divided by 9, produces a quotient of 7 1-9, 
which nearly expresses the intensity of light on Mercury 
compared with that on the earth. Or, more accuratelyi 
thus: Mercury is 36,880,000 of miles from the sun, the 
square of which is 1,360,134,400,000,000 : the earth is distant 
95,000,000, the square of which is 9,025,000,000,000,000. 
Divide this last square by the first, and the quotient is about 
6|, which is very nearly the proportion of light on this 
planet. As the apparent diameter of the sun is Ukewise lA 
proportion to the square of the distance, the inhabitants of

In regard to the temperature of Mercury, if the intensity of 
huU were supposed to be governed by the same law as the in- 
tensity of light, the heat in this planet would, of course, b* 
nearly seven times greater than on the earth. Supposing ^e. 
average temperature of our globe to be fifty degrees of Fahren- 
heit's thermometer, , the average temperature on Mercury 
would be 333 degrees, or 121 degrees above the heat of boil- 
ing water ; a degree of heat sufficient to melt sulphur, to make 
nitrous acid boil, uid to dissipate into vq)our every volatile 
compound. But we have no reason to conclude that the de- 
gree of sensible heat on any planet is in an inverse proportion, 
to its distance from the sun. We have instances of the con- 
trary on our own globe. On the top of the highest range of 
the Andes, in South America, there is an intense cold at all 
times, and their summits are covered with perpetual snows,- 
while in the plains immediately adjacent the inhabitants fed all 
the efifects of the scorching rays of a tropical son. The son^ 
during our summer in the northern hemisphere, is more than 
three millions of miles farther ^m us than in winter ; and al- 
^ugh the obliquity of his rays is partly the cause of the cold 
felt m winter when he is nearest ns, yet it is not the aUy 
etnse ; for w« find that the cold in New-Toik and Pflansyl-' 



T&MBLkTum ow Ksaoiiur. 71 

'fatiia is more Intense itt winter thsn in Scotland, although the 
'mm rises from ten to sixteen decrees higher aboye the hori- 
son in the former case than in ue latter. Besides, we find 
that the heat of summer in the southern hemi^here, when the 
9wn is nearest to the earth, is not so great as in the summer of 
coRes{X)nding latttndes in the northern hemisphere. In short, 
did heat depend chiefly on the nearness of the sun or the ob- 
liquity of his rays, we should always have the same^degree of 
heat or cold at the same time of the year, in a uniform circle ; 
which experience proves to be contrary to fact. Xhe degree 
of heat, therefore, on any planet, and on different portions of 
the same planet, mnst depend in part, and perhaps chiefly, on 
the nature of the atmosphere, .and other circumstances con- 
nected with the constitution of the planet, in combination 
with the influence of the solar rays. These rays undoubtedly 
produce heat, but the degree of its intensity will depend on the 
nature of the substances on which they fall ; as we find that 
the same degree of sensible heat is not felt when they fall on 
a piece of iron or marble as when they fall on a piece of wood 
or flannel. 

Merenry was long considered as the smallest primary planet 
in the system ; but the four new planets lately discovered be- 
tween the orbits of Mars and Jupiter are found to be smaller. 
Its diameter is estimated at 3800 miles, and, consequently, its 
surface contains above 32,000,000 of square miles, and its 
solid contents are 17,157,324,800, or more than seventeen 
thousand millions of solid miles ; and if the number of soUd 
miles conUined in the earth, which are 264,000,000,000, be 
divided by this sum, the quotient will be somewhat more than 
fifteen, showing that the earth is above fifteen times lar^ 
than Meroury . Notwithstanding the comparatively diminutive 
size of thu planet, it is capabfo of containing a population 
vipon its surface^ much greater than has ever ^en supported 
on the smface of the earth during any period of its history. 
In makiiig an estimate on this noint I shall take the popula- 
tion of Engknd as a standard. . England contains 50,000 
square miles of surface, and 14,000,000 of mhabitants, which 
is 280 inhabitants for every square mile. The surface of Mer- 
cury contains 82,000,000 of square miles, which is not much 
less than all the habitable |MKts of our globe. At the rate of 
population now stated, it is therefore sufliciently ample to 
contain 8,9jB0yO00,O0O» or. eight tl^ousand nine hundred ai^

I shall only mention further the following particulars in ref- 
erence to this planet. In its refolotion round the ^un, its

Of course it flies 1830 miles every minute, and more than 
thirty miles during every beat of our pulse. The dennty of 
this planet is found by certain physical calculations and inves- 
tiga^ons, founded on the laws of universal emvitation, to be 
nine ^imes that of water, or equal to that of lead ; so that a 
ball of lead 3200 miles in diameter would exactly poise the 
planet Mercury. This density is greater than that of any of 
the other planets, and nearly twice the density of the earth. 
The mass of this planet, or the qiiantUy ofnuiUer it contains, 
when compared with the mass of the sun, is, according to La 
Place, as 1 to 8,026,810, or about the two millionth part ; 
that is, it would require two millions of globes of the sixe and 
depsity of Mercury to weigh one of the size and density of the 
sun. But as Mercury contains a much greater quantity of 
matter in the same bulk than the sun, in pomi of size it would 
require 22,000,000 of globes of the bulk of Mercury to com- 
pose a body equal to tMt of the sun. In consequence of the 
great density of this planet, bodies will have a greater weiffht 
on its surftoe than on the earth. It has been computed, that 
a body weighinff one pound on the earth's surface would 
weigh one pound eight and a half drachms on the surface of 
Mercury. If the centrifugal ifbrce of this planet were sus-

nil towards the sun, as a stone when thrown iqmord foils to

Oeneral Appearances and apparent Motunu of Venus.-^ 
Hiis planet, as already noticed, is only seen for a short time, 
either alter sunset in the evening, or in the momine before

OS 



never seen at midnight, as all the other planets may be, with 
the exception of Mercury. .It never appears to recede further 
from the sun than forty-seven degrees, or about half the dis- 
tance from the honxon to the senidi. Of course, it ww$ 
never seei\ rising in the east or even shining in the sooth 
after the sun had set in the west, as happens in regard to afii 
the other heavenly bodies, with the reception now stated. 

When this planet, after emerging from the solar ray*» i* 
first seen in the evening, it appears very near the horizon 
about twenty minutes afler sunset, and continues visible only 
for a veiy short time, and descends below the horizon not far 
from the point where the sun went down. Every succeeding 
day its apparent distance from the sun increases ; it rises to a 
higher elevation, and continues a longer time above the hori- 
zon. Thus it sppears to move f^adually eastward from the 
sun for four or five months, till it arrives at the point of its 
greatest elongation, which seldom exceeds ferty-eeven de- 
ffrees, when it appears for some time stationary ; after whieh 
It appears to commence a retrograde motion from east to 
west, but with a much greater degree of apparent velocity ; 
approaching every day nearer the sun, and continuing a shorter 
time above the horizon, till, in the course of two or thiee 
weeks, it appears lost in the splendour of the solar rays, and 
is no longer seen in the evening sky till more than nine or 
ten months have elapsed. About eight or ten days after it 
has disappeared in tne evening, if we look at the eastern sky 
in the morning, a little before sunrise, we shall see a bright 
star very near the horizon, which was not previously to bo 
seen in that qnarter ; this is the planet Venus, wmch hae 
passed its inferior eonjunction with the sun, and has now 
moved to the westward of him, to make its appearance as tho 
morning 9tn, It now appears every succeeding day to mov» 
pretty rapidly from the sun to the westward, tiU it arrives at 
the point of its greatest elongation, between 45^ and 48^ die* 
tant from the sun, when it again appears stationary ; anft then 
returns e&stwafd, with an apparently slow motion, till it ia 
again immersed in the sun*s rajrs, and arrives at its saperiof 
conjunction, which happens aftvr the hqpse of aUwt fiin» 
months from the time of being first seen in the momng. B^l 
the planet is not visible to t£» naked eye all this tint on aof

This diversity of motions and phases, as formeily stated^ 
iwrves to prove the truth of the system, now universally re- 
ceived, which places the sun in the centre, and the eartk 
beyond the orbit of Venus. In order to illustn^te this point 
to the astronomical tyro in the most convincing manner, I 
have frequently used the following plan. With the aid of 
t planetarium, and by means of an ephemeris or a fiautical 
almanac, I place the earth and Venus in their true positions 
on the planetarium, and then desire ^e' learner to place hit 
eye in a line with the balls representing Venus and the earthy 
and to mark the phase of Venus, as seen from the earth, 
whether gibbous, a half moon, or a crescent. I then adjust 
an equatorial teXeecope (if the observation be in the daytime), 
and, pointing it to Venus, show him this planet toith the same 
phase m the heavens; an experiment which never fails to 
|>lease and to produce conviction.

One practical use of the above observations is, that they 
may leaid to the determination of the difference (if any) be- 
tween the polar and equatorial diameters of this planet, which 
point has never yet been determined. It is well known that 
the earth is of a ^heioidal figure, having its polar shorter 
than its equatorial diameter. Apiter, Mars, and Saturn have 
also been ascertained to be oblate spheroids, and the propor- 
tion between their equatorial and polar diameters has been 
pretty accurately determined. As Venus is found to have a 
rotation round her aids, as these planets have, it is reasonable 
to conclude that she is of a similar figure. It is impossible, 
howevor, to doCermine this point when she is hi those posi- 
tisos hi which ^ has generally been viewed ; as at such 
tines rim awiimiBs either a gibbous phase", the i^rm of a half 
moon, or that of a crescent, in neither of which cases can the 
fcwo diaiaelefs be measured. I am therefore of opinion that, 
at some future conjunction, when her geocentric latitude is 
considerible, with a telescope of a high maenifying power, fur- 
nished '^th a micrometer, this point might oe ascertained. If 
the planet is then viewed at a high latitude, and the skv se- 
rene, its disk wHl appear sufficiently luminous and well de- 
fined for this purpose 1 free of that glare and tremulous aspect 
it generally exhibits when near the horizon, which makes it 
appear larger than it ought to do, and prevents its margin 
from being accurately distinguished.

Sufipoted SaUUlU of Vemu. — Several astronomers have 
been of opinion that Venus is attended with a satellite, al-

The celebrated Cassini, who discovered the rotation of Mars,

'* 1686, August 18, at fifteen minutes past four m the

like phenomenon, wmdi resembled the phase of Venus, en 
January 25th, 1672, firom fifty-two minutes after six in the* 
morning to two minutes after seven, when the brightness of 
the twilight caused it to disa^>ear. Venus was thm hemed^ 
and this phenomenon, the diameter of which was neaily a 
fourth part ai the diameter of Venus, was of the same shape. 
It was distant from the southern hotn o^ Venus a diameter s>f 
Venus on the western side. In these two observations I 
was in doubt whether it was not a sateUite ef Vennsy ef such 
a consistence as not to be very well fitted to vefleet the h^ 
of the sun, snd wfaich^ in magnitude, bece neariy tho same pro-

A similar phenomenon is described as having beett seen by 
Baudouin, Montaigne, Rodkier, Montbairon, and other as* 
tronomers, and, from their observations, the celebnted M. 
Lambert, in the '* Memoirs of the Academy Of Berlin" for

concludes that its period is eleven days, five hours, and thir- 
teen minutes ; the inclination of its orbit to the ecl^tic, 63i^ ; 
its distance from Venus, 06| radii of that planet ; and its 
magnitude, 4-27 of that of Venus, or neariy equal to that of 
our moon. There is a singular consistency in these obeervsp 
tions, which it is difficult to account for if Venus have no 
satellite. Astronomers expected that such a body, if it ex- 
isted, would bo seen as a small dark spot upon. the sun at the 
time of thb tsansito of Venus in 1<791 and 1769; but no 
such phenomenon seems fo have been noticed at those times 
by any of the observers. Lambert, however, maintains, from 
the tables he calculated in relation to this body, that the satel- 
lite, if it did exists might) not have passed over the sun's disk 
at the time of the transits, but he expected that it might be 
seen alone on the Sun when Venus passed near that lumi- 
nary.

It is evident that, if Venus have a satellite, it niust be 
difficult to be seen, and can only be perceived in certain 
favourable positions. It cannot be seen when nearly th» 
whole of its enlightened hemisphere is turned to the earth, on 
account of its great distance at such a time, and its proximity 
to Uie sun; nor could it be -expected to be seen when th* 
planet is near its inferior conjunction, as it would then pre- 
sent to the earth only a vexv slender crescent, besides being 
m the immediate neighbouinood of the sun. The best pes^ 
tion in which such a body might be detected is near the tim*' 
of the planet's greatest elongation, and when it would appear 
about half enlightened. If the plane of its orbit be nearly 
coincident wilh the plane of the planet's oibit, it will be hi^ 
monUy hidden by the interposition of the body of Venus, and- 
■kewise when passing alonff her surface in the opposite point 
of its oihit ; and if one side d this body be unfitted for re- 
jecting much lights it will account in part for its being seldom

ft good tcliromatie teleiooDe, ythkk alwayi libowtA Veinit 
with iiich a satellite, and -that the deception was diteorered

more particularly directed to this point than it has hitbento

of tropical climes, it is not impiobsble that a decisive opinion

the deductions of physical astronomy. It is somewhat probia- 
Ue, reasoning il priori, that Venus, a planet nearly as laige 
as the earth, and in its immediate neighbourhood^ it aocoo^ 
panied by a secondary attendant; 

TrantitM ef Vetm$. — ^This planet, when in certam positions, 
is seen to pass like a round mack spot across the dak of the 
eon. - These Iranstlt, as they are called, are of rareoccunence, 
and take place at intervals of 8 and of 1 1 3 years. If the plana 
of the orbit of Venus exactly coincided with that of the eaith, 
a transit would happen at regular intervals of little more than 
nineteen months ; but as one half pC this planet's orbit is three 
degrees and a half below the plane of the earth's oibit, and 
the other half as nuich sbove it, a transit csn only take piaea 
when it happens to be in one of the node*, or intersections of 
the orbits, about the time of its inferior conjunction. Tliesa 
transits of Venns are phenomena of very great importance in 
astronomy, as it is owing to the observations which have been 
made on them, and the calcolations founded on these obseiw 
▼ations, that the distance Of the sun has been very nearly

In addition to the above, the following facts may be stated : 
Venus revolves in an orbit which is 4^,800,000 of miles in 
circumference in the space of 2^24 days 16 hours ; its rate 
4>f motion is therefore about eighty thousand miles evexy 
Iwur, one thousand three hundred and thirty miles every 
minute, and above- twenty-two miles every second. Its dis- 
tance from the sun is 68 millions of miles ; and its distance 
irom the earth, when nearest ue, is about 27 millions of miles, 
which is the nearest ^proach that any of the heavenly bodies 
(except the moon) make to the earth. Yet this distance, 
when considered by itself, is very great ; for it would require 
a cannon ball six years and three months to move from the 
earth to the nearest point of the orbit of Yenus, although it 
wnn flying every moment at the rate of 600 miles an hour, or

•ad M it is eonqoeted with them in the same flfystem, it may

The earth, though apparently a quiescent body in the centra 

of the heavens, is sttiqpended in empty spac^, surrounded on 

all sides by the celestial luminaries and the spaces of the 

firmament. Though it appears to our view to occupy « space 

lu^ than all the heavenly orbs, yet it is, in fact, almost in* 

fimtely smaller, and holds a rank only with the smaller bodies 

of the universe ; and, alUwugh it appears to the eye of sense 

immovably fixed in the same position, yet it is, in reality, 

flying through the ethereal spaces at the rate of more than 

a thousand miles every minute, as we have already demon* 

atrated. The Jigure of the earth is now ascertained to be 

that of an oblate spheroid, very nearly i^roaching to the figure 

of a globe. ^An orange and a common turnip are oblate sphe^* 

voids, and are frequently exhibited to illustrate the, figure of 

the earth. But they tend to convey an erroneous idea ; for, 

although a spheroid of ten feet diameter were constructed to 

exhibit the true figure of the earth, no eye could distinguisji 

Uie difference between such a spheroid and a perfect globe, 

since the difference of its two diameters woula scaiceqr af «< 

ceed one third of an inch ; whereas, if its diameters bore the 

■ame proportion to each other. as the two diameters of an or 

ange generally do, its polar diameter would be nearly one foot 

three mches s^iorter than its equatorial. 

Before the time of Newton it was never suspected that the 
figure of the earth differed in anjr degree from that of a per- 
fect apbere, excepting the small inequalities produced by the 
mountains and vales. The first circumstance which led to 
the determination of its true fig^e was an accidental expcri* 
ment made with a pendulum near the equator. M. Richer, a 
Flrenchman, in a voyage made to Cayenne, which lies nea^ the 
oquator, found that the pendtdum of his clock no longer made 
ks vibrations so frequently as in the latitude of Paris^and that 
it was absolutely necessary to shorten it in order Jto make it 
agree with the times of the stars passiuff the meridian. Some 
Tears after this, Messrs. Beshayes and Varin, who were sent 
ij the French king to make certain astronomical obserfa* 
tions near the dquator, found that the pendulum at Cayenne 
made 148 vibrations less in a day than at Paris, and tMt hio 
«k>ck was lataided by that means two minutes twenty-eigki^ 



106 D18G0TBET 09 ITS 8FHXEOIDAL FIGUKS. 

feeondi ; and wm obliged to make hit pendahim shorter hf 
two lines, or the sixth part of a Paris inch, in order to make 
the time agree with that deduced from celestial observations. 
Similar enieriments, attended with the same results, were 
made at Martinique, St. Domingo, St. Helena, Ooree, on the 
coast of Africa, and Tarious other places, in all which it was 
Ibund that the alteration was the greatest under the equator, 
and that it dimmished as the observer approached the northern 
latitudes. This discovery, trifling as it may at first sight ap- 
pear, opened a new field of investigatioi)^ to philosophic minds ; 
and there are, perhaps, few facto throughout tne range of science 
fimn wluch so many curious and important facto have been 
deduced. Sir Isaac Newton and M. Huygens were among 
the first who perceived the extensive application of this dis- 
covery, and tne impOStant results to which it might lead, 
Newton, wliose penetrating eye traced the fact thioush aH 
hs bearings and remote consequences, at once perceived that 
the earth must have some other figure than what was com- 
monly supposed, and demonstrated that this diminution of 
weight naturally arises from the earth*s roUtion round its axis, 
which, according to the laws of circular motion, repels all 
Itoavy bodies from the axis of motion ; so that, this motion 
being swifter at the equator than in other parte more remote, 
the weight of bodies must also be lest there than near the 
poles. An heavy bodies, when left to themselves, fall towards 
• the earth in lines perpendicular to the horizon; and, were 
those lines continued, they would all pass through the earth's 
centre. Every part of the earth, therefore, gravitotes towards 
the centre ; and as this force is found to be about S$9 times 
greater than that which arises from the rotation of the earth, 
a certain balance will constantly be maintained between them, 
end the earth will assume such a figure as would naturally 
lesult from the difference of these two opposite forces. FVem 
various considerations and ciicumstonces of this kind, New« 
(on founded his sublime calculations on this subject ; and, ss 
Fontenelle remarks, '* determined the true figure of the earth 
w^out leavinff his elbow-chair." 

Newton and Huygens were both engaged in these investi- 
gtHens at the same time, unknown to each other, but the re- 
•nlto of their calculations were nearly alike. They demons 
etiated, finMn the known laws of gravitation, that the true figure 
of tlM earth was that of an oblate spheroid, flattened at the

nearly correct, it follows that a degree of latitude in the polar 
regions must measure more than a degree near the equator 
To determine this point by actual measurement, it was or-

ter is more than 6000 miles.

Our summer is nearly ei^ht days longer than our winter. 
By summer is meant the time that passes between March 
21 and September 23, or between the vernal and autumnal 
equinoxes; and by winter, the time between September 23 
and March 21, the autumnal wad vernal eipiinoxes. The 
portion of the earth's oibit which lies north df the equinoctial 
contains ld4 degrees^ while that portion which is south of the 
eqjoinoctial contains only 176 degrees,, being eight degrees 
less than the other portion, which is the reason why the snn 
is nearly eifht days longer oh the north of the equator than 
on the south. In our summer the sun's apparent motion is 
thiouffh the six northern signs, Aries, Tamua, Gemini, Can- 
cer, Leo, and Virgo; and in our winter, through the six 
southern. In the former case, from Marrit 21 to September 
iSf the sun is about 186 days 11 hours in passing through 
the northern signs, and only 178 days IS hours in passing 
through the southern signs^ from S^ptamber 23 to Mam

The cause of the variety of the seasons can be exhibited 
with more cleameas and precision by means of machinery than 
by verbal descriptions ; and, therefore, those whose concep- 
tions are not clear and well defined on this subject should 
have recourse to orreries and planetariums, which exhibit the 
celestial motions by wheelwork. There is a small instru- 
ment, called a Tellurian, which has been long manufactured 
fav Messrs. Jones, Holbom, London, which conveys a pretty 
clear idea of the motions and phases of the moon, the incli- 
nation of the earth's axis to the plane of its orbit, and the 
changes of the seasons. It may be procured at different 
prices, from XL 8s. to 4/. 14f. 6d., according to the size and 
tbequantity of the wheelwork. 

The subject of the seasons and the variety of phenomena 
they exhibit have frequently been the themes both of the phi- 
losopher and the poet, who have expatiated on the beauty of 
the contrivance and the benignant effects they produce ; and 
therefore they conclude that other planets enjoy the same vi- 
cissitudes and seasons similar or analogous to Ours. But 
•Uhougb, in the present constitution of owr globe, there aie 
many benign agencies which accompany the revolutions of the 
•eaaonsy and are essential to our happiness in the circum- 
iCaneea in which we now exist, yet it is by no means proba- 
ble that the aeaaoos, as thty nowoperate, formed a part of the 
«rigiBil inangoments of our tenestrial system. Man was at 
lint created in a etate of innocence, and adorned with the imp 
•ft ofhtf MakMT ; and the fiameof nature, we mayoenfidently 

h 



11122 THB BEi.80N8 KOT A PART OF 

flUTOKwe, was so arranged as to contribute in every respeqt 
both to his sensitiye and intellectual enjoyment. But neither 
the horrors of winter, and its dreary aspect in northern climes, 
nor the scorching heats and appalling thunderstorms which 
are experienced in tropical climates, are congenial to the rank 
and circumstances of beings untainted with sin and endowed 
with moral perfection. Such physical evils and inconveni- 
ences as the change of seasons occasionally produces appear 
to be only adapted to man in his present state of moral deg- 
radation. In the primeval state of the world it is not unlikely 
that the axis of the earth had a different direction from 'what 
it has at present, and that, instead of scorching heats and 
piercing colds, and the gloom and desolations of winter, there 
was a more mild and equable temperature, and something ap- 
proaching to what the poets call " a perpetual spring." We 
are assured, from the records of sacred history, that the ori- 
ginal constitution of the earth has undergone a considerable 
change and derangement : its strata were disrupted, ** the 
fountains of the great deep were broken up," and a flood of 
waters covered toe tops of the loftiest mountains ; the ef- 
fects of which are still visible in almost every region of the 
globe. At that memorable era, it is highly probable, those 
changes were introduced which diversify the seasons and 
produce those alarming phenomena and destructive effects 
which we now behold ; but as man advances in his moral, in- 
tellectual, and religious career, and in proportion as his men- 
tal and moral energies are made to bear on the renovation of 
the world, he has it in his power to counteract or meliorate 
many of the physical evils which now exist. Were the hab- 
itable parts of*^ the earth universally cultivated, its marshes 
drained, and its. desolate wastes reduced to order and vegeta^ 
ble beauty by the hand of art, and replenished with an' indus- 
trious and enlightened population, there can be little doubt 
that the seasons would be considerably meliorated, and many 
physical evils prevented with which we are now annoyed. 
And all this is within the power of man to accomplish, pro* 
Tided he chooses to direct his wealth, and his intellectual and 
moral energies, into this channel. If these remarks have any 
foundation in truth, then we ought not to imagine that the 
earth is a standard by which we are to judge of the state of 
other planetaxpr worlds, or that they are generally to be tiewed 
w having a diversity of seasons sundar to ours

. The following facts, in addition to the preceding, may be 
noted in relation to the earth : Under the equator, a pendulum, 
of a certain form and length, makes 86,400 -vibrations in a 
mean solar day ; but, when transported to London, the same 
pendulum makes 86,535 vibrations in the same time. Hence 
it is concluded that the intensity of the force urging the pen- 
dulum downward^ at the equator is to that at C>ndon as 
86,400 to 86,535, or as 1 to 1 00315 ; or, in other words, 
that a mass of matter at the equator weighing 10,000 pounds, 
exerts the same pressure on the ground as 10,03 1} of the 
tame /Kmn^ transported to London would exert there. If 
the gravity of a body at the equator be 1, at the poles it will 
be 1 00569, or about the 1-194 part heavier ; that is, a body 
weighing 194 pounds at the equator would weigh 195 pounds 
1^ the north pole ; so that the weight of bodies is increased 
as we advs^ce from the equator to the poles, owing to the 
polar parts being nearer the centre of the earth than the equa- 
torial, and the centrifugal force being diminished. It is this 
variation of the action of gravity in different latitudes thai 
causes the same pendulum to vibrate slower at the equator 
than in other places, as stated above. For a pendulum to os- 
cillate seconds at the equator, it must be thirty- nine inches in 
length ; and at the poles, thirty-nine and one fifth inches. 

The tropical year, or the time whidi the sun (or the earth) 
takes in moving through the twelve signs of the ecliptic, from 
one equinox to the same equinox again, is three hundred and 
sixty-Qve days, five hours, forty-eight minutes, and fifty-one 
seconds, lliis is the proper or natiiral year ; because it al- 
ways keeps the same seasons to, the same months. The «i* 
derad year is the space of time the sun takes in passing from 
any fixed star till it returns to the same star a^in. U con- 
sists of three hundred and sixty-five days, six hours, nine 
minutes, and eleven and a half seconds, being twenty minutes 
and twenty and a half seconds longer than the true solar year. 
This difference is owing to the regression of the equinoctial 
points, which is fifty seconds of a aegree every year ; and, to 
pass over this space, the sun requires twenty minutes and 
twenty and a half seconds. The earth moves in an elliptical 
orbit, whose eccentricity ^ or distance of its foci from the cen- 
tre, is 1,618,000 miles : that is, the ellipse or oval in which 
it moves is double the eccentricity, or 3,236,000 mileis longer 
Ui one direction than it is in anotheri which is the reason 



1^ . THS PI.ANBT MARS. 

thtt the son is further, from us at one season of the year than 
at another. This is ascertained from the Taiiation of the 
apparent diameter of the sun. Ahout the 1st of Janairy, 
when he is nearest the earth, the apparent diameter is thirty- 
two minutes, thirty-five seconds ; and on the 1st of July, 
when he is most distant, it is only thirty-one minutes, thirty- 
one seconds. This proves that the earth has a slower mo- 
tion in one part of its orbit than . in another. In January it 
moves at the rate of about 69,600 miles an hour, Imt in 
July its rate of motion every hour is only about 66,400 miles ; 
a difference of more than 8000 miles an hour. 

IT. OF THE PLANIT 1U«8. 

The earth is placed, in the eobr system, in a position 
between the orbits of Venus and Mars. The two planets, 
Mercury and Venus, which are placed vrUhin the orbit of 
the earth, and whose -orbits lie between it and the son, aris 
termed the inferior planets. Those whose orbits lie beyond 
the orbit of the earth, at a greater distance from the sun, as 
Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, and Uranus, are termed euperior planets. 
The motions and aspects of all the superior planets, as seen 
from the earth, differ considerably from those which are ex- 
hibited by the inferior. In the first place, the inferior planets 
are never seen but in the neighbourhood of the son, none of 
them ever appearing beyond forty-eight degrees fi^mi that In- 
minaiy ; whereas the superior planets appear at all distances 
firom the sun, even in the opposite quarter of the heavens, or 
180 degrees from the point in which the sun may happen to be 
placed. Tliis could not possibly happen unless their orbits 
were exterior to that of the earth, and the earth placed at 
such times between them and the sun. In the next place, the 
inferior planets, when viewed through telescopes, exhioit, at de- 
ferent times, all the phases of the nibon ; but the superior plan- 
ets never appear either homed or in the shape of a half moon. 
The planets Jupiter, Saturn, and Uranus never appear in 
any other shape than round, or with full enligfatenea hemi- 
spheres. This circumstance of itself furnishes a proof that 
we see these planets always in a direction not ^irw remote 
from that in which they are illuminated 1^ the sour rays ; 
and, consequently, that we occupy a station which is never 
very far removed from the centre of their orbits. It proves^ 
in other words, that the path of the earth loimd the aim ia en*

What has now been stated in regard to the apparent motions

Saturn, and of Saturn than of Uranus. The longer the 
periodic time or annual revolution of a superior planet, the 
more frequent are its stations and retrogradations ; they are 
less in qusntity, but continue a longer time. The mean arc

From what has been now stated, it is evident that this plan- 
et will present a very different aspect as to size and splen- 
dour in different parts of its orbit. When nearest to the earth, 
it appears with a surface twenty-five times larger than it does 
at Its greatest distance^ and seems to yie with Jupiter in ap« 
parent magnitude and splendour. But, when vergmff towards 
Its conjunction with the sun, it is almost imperceptible. And 
this is one proof, among others, of the truth of the Copemican- 
system. All its motions, stations, and direct and retrograde 
movements, and the times in which they happen, exactly ac- 
cord with its position in the system and the motion of the 
earth, as a planet between the orbits of Venus and Mars. 
Whereas, were the earth supposed to be the centre of this 
planet's motion, according to the Ptolemaic hypothesis, it 
would be impossible to account for any of the phenomena 
above stated. 

^ The orbit of Mars is 901,064,000, or more than 900 mil- 
Kons of miles in circumference. Through this space it moves 
in one year and 822 days, or in 16,488 hoars. Conse(|uent!y, 
its rate of motion is 54,649 miles every hour, which is more 
than a hundred times the greatest Yoloci^^ of a cannos ball 
when it leaves the mouth of the cannon. Ine diurnal rotation 
of this planet, or its revolution round its axis, is accomplished 
in twenty-four hours, thirty-nine minutes, twenty-one seconds, 
which is about two thirds of an hour longer than onr day. 



MARS» A8 SEEN THROUGH TELESCOPftg. ISl 

Tliis period of rotation was first ascertained by Cassini, from 
the motion of certain spots on its surface, which I shall after- 
ward describe. Its axis is inclined, to the plain of its orbit 
in an angle of thirty degrees, eighteen minutes, which is nearly 
seven degrees more inclined froni the perpendicular tl^an that 
of the earth. This motion is in the same direction as the ro- 
tation of the earth, namely, from 'west to east. The inclina- 
tion of the orbit of Mars to that of the earth is one degree, 
fifty-one minutes, six seconds, so that this planet is never so 
imich as two degrees either horth or south of the ecliptic. The 
orbit of Mars is considerably ecc«7i<m. Its eccentricity is no 
less than 13,463,000 miles, or about 1-21 of its diameter, 
which is more than eight times the eccentricy. of the orbit of 
the earth. Hence it follows, that Mars, when in opposition 
to the sun, may be nearer the earth by a consideraUe number 
of millions of miles at one time than at another, when he 
happens to be about his perihelion^ or nearest distance from 
^e sun at such opposition. On the 27th of August, 1719, 
this planet was in such a position, being in opposition within 
two and a half degrees of its perihelion, and nearer to the earth 
than it had been for a long period before ; so that its magni- 
tude and brightness were so much increased that, by common 
spectators, it was taken for a new star.

Atmosphere of Mars. — f'rom the gi:adual diminution of tbe 
light of the fixed stars when they approach near the disk of 
Mars, it has been inferred that this planet ib surrounded with 
an atmosphere of great extent. Although the extent of this 
atmosphere has been much overratedvy^t it is generally ad- 
mitted by astfdHOmerS that an atmosphere of considerable 
density and elevation exists. Both Gassini and Roemer ob- 
served a star, at six minutes from the disk of Mars^ become 
so faint before it was covered by the planet that it could not 
be seen even with a three^feet telesicope ; "which, in all prob* 
ability, was caused, by the'light of the star being obscured by 
passing through the dense part of the .atmosphere of the, 
planet. It is . doubtless owing to this circumstance that 
Mars presents so ruddy an appearance, tfiore so than any 
other planet or star in the nocturnal sky. When a Jbeam of 
light passes thiough a dense medium, its colour inclines to 
IM, the other rays bein^ partly reflected or absorbed. Thus 
the morning and even^ig clouds are generally tinged with 
red, and the. sun, moon, and stars, when near the horizon, 
either rising or setting, uniformly. assume a ruddy aspect, be*, 
cause their hgfat then passes through the lower and denseif 
part of our atmosphere. When the light of the sun passes 
thfough the atmosphere of Mars, the most refrangible colours, 
aueh as the violet, will be partly absorbed ; and before the 
reflected rays reach the earth, they must again pass through 
the atmosphere of the planet, and be deprived of another por- 
tion of the most refirangible rays ; and, consequently, the ted. 
rays will predominate, and the planet assume a dull red 
colour. . This I conceive to be the chief reason why I could 
never perceive Mars in the daytime, even when in the most, 
&vau^i)le. position, so distinctly as Jupiter, although thei]

^aatdtf of solar light which falls on this planet is mors than 
«itev«n tim^s greater'tfaan what falls on Jupiter ; which seems 
!• indicate-that Jupiter is snmMinded witn a less dense and 
more transparent atmosphere. Sir W. Herschel, though he 
aneslions we aecnracy of -some of the obserrations of the 
Jimness -caused by the appulses of the 'fixed stars to" this 
planet, yet admits that it has a considerable atmosphere. 
'^For," says he,' '* besides the permanent spots on its sur- 
ftoe, I have often noticed occasional changes of partial bridht 
belts, and ali90 once a darkish one in- a pretty high latitaae : 
and Uiese alterations we can hardly ascribe to any other cause 
than the variable disposition o( clouds and vapours floating m 
the atmosphere of the planet."

The Planet Juno. — The nei;t planet in the order of the 
system is Juno. Its distance from the sun is estimated st 
S54 millions of miles. The circumference of its orbit is 
1696 millions of miles. Through this circuit it moves in four 

ears and 188 days, at the rate of 41,860 miles eveiy hour. 

'ts diameter, according to the estimate of Schroeter, is 1426 
Knglish miles. Its surfa;ce will therefore contain six mill- 
ions, three hundred and eighty thousand square miles, and a 
population of one thousand, seven hundred and eighty-six 
millions, which is more than double the number of the earth's 
inhabitants. The otbit of Juno is inclined to the ecliptic in 
an angle of thhrteen degrees, three minutes. Its eccentricity 
is 63,688,000 miles, so that its greatest distance from the 
sun is 816,968,000 miles, w^iile ito least distance is onlv 
189,792,000. Its apparent diameter as seen from the earth n 
little more than three seconds. This planet is of a reddish 
eolour, and is free from any nebulosity ; yet the observations 
of Schroeter render it probable that it has an atmosphere 
more dense than that of any of the old planets of the system. 
A remarkable variation m the brilliancy of this planet has 
been observed by this astronomer, which he attributes to 
changes that are somg on in its atmosphere, and thinks it not 
improbable that these changes may arise from a diurnal rota- 
tionpeifbrmed in twenty-seven hours.

3. The orbits of seteral of the new planets cross each other. 
— ^This is a very singular and unaccountable circumstance in 
regard to the planetary orbits. It had been long observed 
that comets, in traversins the heavens in every direction, 
crossed the orbits of the planets ; but, before the discovery of 
Pallas, no such anomaly was found throughout the system of 
the planets. For the orbits of all the other planets approach 
so nearly to circles, and are separated from each other by so 
many millions of miles, that there is no possibility of such 
intersection takinc place. . The followinff diaffram represents 
the intersection of the orbits of Ceres and Pallas. (See Fig, 
XLVIII.) 

The central circle represents the sun ; the two next circles 
the orbits of the earth and Mars ; and the two outer circles, 
crossing each other, those of Ceres and Pallas. In conse- 
quence of this intersection of their orbits, there is a possibility^ 
especially if the periods of their revolutions were somewhat 
more different from each other, that the two planets might 
happen to strike against each other were they to meet at the 
pomts A and B, where the orbits intersect, a very singular 
contingence in the planetary system. It is owing to the very 
great eccentricity of the orbit of Pallas that it crosses the 
orbit of Ceres. It is several millions of miles nearer the sun 
in its perihelion (or at ii, Fig. XLVII.) than Ceres, when in 
the same pomt of its orbit, nut when Pallas is in its aphelion 
(or at B, Fi^. XLVII.), its distance from the sun is several 
millions of tunes greater than that of Ceres in the same point 
of its orbit Suppose its aphelion at C, Fig. XLVIII. ; it is 
farther firom the sun than Ueres, and nearer at D its perihe- 
lion. 1^ same things happen in the case of the other two

it is easy to foresee the catastrophe that would take place. 
If the collision of two large ships, sailing at the rate of ten 
miles an hour, he so dreadful as to shatter their whole frame 
and sink them in the deep, what a tremendous shock would 
be encountered by the impulse of a ponderous globe, movinff 
at the rate of forty thousand miles an hourl A universu 
disruption of their parts and a derangement of their whole 
constitution would immediately ensue ; their .axes of rotation 
would be changed ; their courses in their orbits altered ; fraff* 
ments of their substance tossed about through the surround- 
ing void, and the heavens above would appear to run into 
confusion. Though we cannot affirm that such an event is 
impossible or will never happen, yet we are sure it can never 
take place without the permission and appointment of Him 
who at first set these bodies in motion, and who superintends 
both the greatest and the most minute movements of the uni- 
verse. 

4. Another peculiarity in respect to these planets is, that 
thetf revolve nearly at the same mean distancee from the sun. 
The mean distance of Juno is 254 millions of miles ; that of 
Ceres, 263,908,000; and that of Pallas, 262,901,000, which 
is almost the same as Ceres. This is a very different ar- 
rangement from ^t of the other planets, whose mean dis- 
tances are immensely different from each other ; Mars beinf 
50 millions of miles from the orbit of the earth, and 80 mil> 
ions from the orbits of any of the new planets ; Jupiter, 270 
millions from Pallas ; Saturn, 412 millions from Jupiter ; and 
Uranus, 900 millions from Saturn. Except in the case of the 
new planets, the planetary system appears constructed on the 
most ample and magnificent scale, corresponding to the un- 
limited range of infinite space of which it forms a part. 

5. These new planetary bodies perform their revolutions in 
nearly the same periods^ The period of Vesta is 3 years, 
7} months ; that of Juno, 4 years, 4^ months ; of Ceres, 4 
years, 7^ months : and of PaUas, 4 years, 7^ months. So 
-that there are only three months of difference between the

6. Another singularity is, that these bodies art all mueh 
smaller than the other pUmets. Mercury was long considered 
as the smallest primary planet in ^e system, but it is nearly 
four times larger in surface than Ceres, and contains eight 
times the number of solid miles. Mars, the next snudlest 

f»lanet, is seventeen times larger than Ceres ; and Jupiter, the 
argest of the planets, is 170)000 times larger than Ceres when 
their cubical contents are compared. The planets Yesta and 
Juno are smaller tbui Ceres, and Pallas is only a small degree 
larger. It is probable that all these four bodies are less in size 
than the secondary planets, or the satellites of Jupiter, Sa- 
turn, and Uranus. 

Conclusions respecting the Nature of the New Planets,'^ 
The anomalies and peculiarities of these bodies, so very dif- 
ferent from the order and arrangement of the older planets, 
open a wide field for reflection and speculation. Having been 
accustomed to survey the jplanetaiy system as a «cene of pro^ 
portion, harmony, ^and order, we can scarcely admit that these 
bodies move in the same paths,, and are arranged In the same 
order as when the system was originally constructed by its 
Omnipotent Contriver. As we know that changes have taken 
place in' our sublunary region since our globe fist came from 
the hands of its Creator, so it is not contrary either to reason 
or observation to suppose that changes and revolutions, evea 
on an ample scale, may take place among the celestial orbs. 
We have no reason to believe in the *' incorruptibility'' of the 
heavenly orbs, as the ancients imagined, for the planets an 
demonstrated to be opaque globes as well as the earth ; they 
are diversified with mountains and vales, and, in all probability, 
the materials which compose their suiiaees and interior are 
not very different from the substances which constitute the 
component parts of the earth. I have alraady allnded to th/$ 
opinion of Dr. Olben, that th« new plansU we only the fta|^

mtiito of a krger pboMt wliich had been burst taonder hf

tMrts. HoweTer 8ttan|ne this opinion may at first sight appear, 
It ought not to be considered as either very improlwbie or ez- 
tnmrant. We ail profess to adnut, on the authority of 
ReTelation, that the earth was amnged in peifeet ozder and 
beauty at its first creation ; and on the same authority we be-

of heaTon were opened, and the fountains of the great deep 
broken up," and that'a flood of waters ensued which covered 
the tops of the loftiest mountains, which transformed the earth 
into one boundless ocean, and buried the immense myriads oi 
its population in a watery giaTO. This was a catastrophe at 
tremendous and astonishing as the bursting asunder of a large 
planet. Ai&ough i^ysical agents may have been employed in 
either case to produce the effect, yet we must admit, in con«> 
aistency with the DiTine perfections, that no such OTents could

and that, when they do happen, wfaaterer appalling or lisas* 
tJEOUs flifoeta- they may- produce, they are in perfect consistency 
with the moral laws by which his.uniTersai government is di- 
rected. 

We know that a moral revolution has taken place among

be esseaftisUy the same througbcmt every part of the boundless

convolsioBs of our globe, may have operated in the regions to

purposes. In the course of the astronomical discoveries of 
the two preceding centuries, views of the universe have been

The hypothesis of the bursting of a large planet between 
Mars and Jupiter accounts in a ^at measure, if notentirely, 
for the anomalies and apparent irregularities which have been 
observed in the system of the new planets ; and if this sup-

yet discovered, for the singular phenomena which these planets 
exhibit. Sir David Brewster, who has entered into some par- 
ticular discussions on this subject, after stating the remarka-

On the l^th of April, 1803, an extraordinary shower of 
■tones happened at L' Aigle, in Normandy. About one o'clock, 
the sky being almost serene, a rolling noise like that of thun- 
der was heani, and a fiery globe of uncommon splendour was 
seen, which moved through the atmosphere with great rigid- 
ity. Some moments after there was heard at L' Aigle, and 
for thirty leagues round in every direction, a violent explosion, 
which lasted five or six mintites ; after which was heard a 
dreadful rumbling like the beating of a drum. In the whole 
district there was heard a hissing noise like that of » stone 
discharged from a sling, and a great many mineral masses^ 
exactly similar to those distinguished by the name of meteor 
»toneSf were seen to fall. The largest of these stones weighed 
seventeen pounds and a half. The Vicar of. St. Michaers 
observed one of the stones faU with a hissing noise at the feet 
of *his niece in the courtyard of his parsonage, and that it re- 
bounded more than a foot from the pavement. When it was 
taken up and examined, it was fouxid to resemble the others 
in every respect. As a wire manufiusturer was working with 
his men in the open air, a stone grazed his arm and fell at his 
feet, but it was se hot that, on attempting to take it up, he in- 
stantly let it fall again. The celebrated Biot was deputed by 
Svemment to rspair to the spot and collect all the authentic 
;ts in reietion te this phenomenon, an account of which was 
afterward publiahed in a long memoir. He found that ahnost 
all the residents of twenty hamlets declared that they were 
eyewitnesses of the showor of stones which was darted from 
the meteor. The interior parts of these stones resembled 
those of sll the meteorites analyzed by Messrs. Howard and 
Vanqoelin, such as those described above. They all contain 
•ilica, magnesia, oxyd of iron, luckel, and sulphur, in vanous

PROPOBTIOM W lilGHT OV SATURN* 18$ 

miles every hour. The period of its rotation was for a long 
time unknown. About acentury i^, it was conjectured by 

Sme astronomers that it was accomplished in about ten or 
Bven hours. It was not, however^ till iSir W. Herschel a^ 
plied his powerful telescopes to Saturn that its rotation was 
Accurately determined. By certain dark spots which he per- 
ceived on its disk, and by their change of position, he ascer- 
taiiied that the diurnal rotation is performed in ten hours, six- 
teen minutes, and nineteen seconds.* It is remarkable that 
La Place, from physical considerations, had calculated the 
rotation of Saturn to be nearly the same as above stated, be- 
fore Herschel had determined it by direct observation. The 
iDtatioit is performed on an axis perpendicular to the plane of 
the ring. -The ciicamference of Saturn being 248,000 miles, 
the parts about the equator will tnove at the rate of 24,000 
miles an hour. Its year will consist of 25,150 days, or periods 
of its diurnal rotation. 

Proportum of Light on Saturn. — >This planet being about 
9 1-2 times farther from the sun than the earth, it will receive 
only the one ninetieth of the light which we receive ; for the 
square of 9 1-2 is equal to 90 1-4. This quantity of light, 
however, is equal tQ the light which would be reflected' from 
a thousand ^full moons suoh as ours ; and there cAn be litUe 
doubt that the beings that reside in this planet have their 
organs of vision so constructed as to be perfectly adapts to 
the quantity of li^ht they receive ; and, by such an adapta- 
tion, all the objects around them may appear as. splendidly 
enligihtened, and their colours as vivid as they do on the globe 
on which we live. The spparent diameter of the snn, as seen 
from Saturn, is three minutes, twenty-two seconds ; but his 
mean apparent diameter, as seen from the earth, is equal to 
thirty-two minutes, three seconds. This proportion of sixe 
in which the sun appears from the earth and from Saturn is 
represented in the following figure, in which the small circle

From want of correct views on this subject, several foolish

In a late number of a popular and extensively circulated jour-

IX. ON THE PLANET USANUS. 

Since the time of Newton, when the physical causes of the 
celestial motions began to be studied ana investigated, astron- 
omers have had their attention directed to the power or in- 
fluence wh)ch the planetary bodies exert upon each other 
This power is termed attraction or gravitation, uid is inherent 
in all material substances, so far as oer knowledge extends. 
It is exerted in proportion to the quantity of matter and the 
Stances of the respective bodies ; the planets, in their near- 
est aj^prdach to each other, cansing some slight deviations in

oiUts "ftBd nwtlonfl. Some distoibciieM or ineqnaUtiM 
in the motions of Jupiter and Saturn, which could not be ao* 
counted for from the mutual action oif these planets, led cer- 
tain astronomers to conclude that another planet of consider- 
able maomtude existed beyond the orbit of Saturn, by th» 
action of which these irregularities were produced. It was 
not, however, till near the close of the eighteenth century that 
this happy •conjectuve was lealized end oonfirmed. To th« 
bte Sir W. fierschel astionemy is indebted for discoveripg % 
new primary planet, whicb had been pvevioosly unknown t» 
all astronomers. 

Hiis illustrious aotraoeiner, when lesiding ki Bath, had 
constructed reflecting telescopes «f e largrer size and with 
higher poweia than any that bad been preyiously in use, and 
bad doTOted his unwearied attention to celestial observations* 
While pursuing a design vriuch be bad formed, of making 
minute obserrationa on every region of the heavena, on the 
13th of March, 1761, while examining, with one of his best 
telescopes, the constellation of Gemini, he observed a star 
near the foot of Castor, the light of which appeared to differ 
considerably from that of the neighbouring stars, or those 
which he round described in catalogues. On applying a 
kigher magnifying power it appeared evidently to increase in 
diametet ; and two days afterward he perceived that its place 
was changed, and that it had moved a little from its foimeir 
position. From these circumstauces he concluded that it waa 
a comet, and sent an account of it as such to the astronomer 
wyal. As a comet, however, it seemed particularly singular 
that no tail or nebulous appearance eould be perceived ; on 
the contrary, it was found to show with a faint steady light, 
eomewhat paler than that of Jupiter. The account of this 
^scovery soon tqpread throughout Europe, and was confirmed 
Dy observations made at Paris, Vienna, Milan, Pisa, Berlin, 
and StockhoUn. Theater was for some time generally con- 
sidered as an. extraordinary comet, free of all nebulosity, and 
astronomers were occupied in determining the paabolic ele- 
ments of its' course. " The President B^hard de Saron, of 
the Academy «f Sciences of Paris, andXiOxel, an astronomer 
of St. PeterriMug, who was in London at the time, were the 
first .who discovered its circular fontt and calcnlated the 
^dimensions of its orbit. It was no longer doubted that Her- 
•chel't star was a new planet ; and all suisequent observations

t«nfied this unexpected resalt.'** We have heie a ttrikiii|p

Since its discovery to the present time, it has not yet moved

When the motion of this new planet was calculated, the 
points of the heavens which it had successively occupied 
during the preceding century could be pointed out ; and it 
occurred to some astronomers that it might possibly have been 
observed before, though not known to be a planet. Mr. Bode, 
of Berlin, who had just published a work containing all the 
catalogues of zodiacal stars which had appeared, was induced 
to consult these catalogues in order to discover whether any 
star marked by one astronomer, and omitted by another, 
might not be the new planet in question. In the course of 
iSiis inquiry he found that the star No. 9S4 in Mayer's cata* 
Yogue had been unobserved by others, and observed only once 
by Mayer himself, so that no motion could have been perceiv- 
ed by him. On this Mr. Bode immediately directed his tele- 
scope to that part of the heavens where he might expect to 
fod it, but without success. At the same time he found, by 
calculation, that its apparent place in the year 1756 ought to 
have been that of Mayer*s star, and this was one of the years 
in which he was busied in his observations ; and, on farther 
inquiry, it was found that the sUr 964 had been discovered 
by Mayer on the 15th of September, 1756 ; so that it is now 
believed that this star was the new planet of Herschel. It 
appears likewise that this stor was seen several times by 
Flamstead, the astronomer royal, in the year 1690 ; once by

fil4 TEHPERATURB OF URANUd. 

have no valid reason to conclude that the degree of heat on 
the surfaces of the different planets is inversely proportional 
to the sqnaies of their resptective distances fronv the sun. TTie 
san is to be considered chiefly as the great storehouse of ligkif 
and it may likewise be viewed as the great agent in the pio- 
doction of heat, without supposing ft to be an enormous mass 
of fire, which the common opinion seedis to take for granted. 
Its rays produce heat chiefly by exciting an insensible action 
between caloric and the particles of matter contained in bodies ; 
and caloric appears to be a substance universally diffused 
throughout nature. If the degree of heat were in proportion 
to the distance from the sun, why should the upper reffions of 
the atmosphere be so intensely cold? Why should me tops 
of lofty mountains be crowned with perpetual snows, whue 
the plains below are scorched with heati Why should an 
intense cold be felt in the latitude of 40*^, when a compam- 
tive.miklness is experienced in the latitude of 66° 1 In the 
state lof Connecticut, North America, in January, 1835, the 
thermometer ranged from minus 25° to 27° of Fahrenheit ; 
while in Scotland, during the same period, it was seldom so 
low as .the freezing point. But as I nave already thrown out 
some remarks on this subject 'when describing the planet 
Mercury, I need not enlarge (see page 70). In Order to form, 
correct ideas. of the distribution of ^eat. among the planetarj 
bodies, we have only to suppose that the Creator has propor- 
tioned the quantity of caloric (or that which produces sensi- 
ble heat) to the distance at which every planet is placed irom 
the sun, so that a large quantity exists m Saturn and a smaller 
quantity in Mercury. If, therefore, the quantity of caloric 
connected with Uranus be in proportion to its distance from 
the sun, there may be as much warmth experienced in that 
distant region of the solar system as i^ the mildest parts of 
our temperate zones. So that we are under no necessity of 
associating the frigid and gloomy ideas of the poet with our 
contemphitions of this expansjve globe. At all events, we 
may rest assured that the Creator, whose wisdom is infinite 
in Its resources, and whose "tender mercies are over all his 
works,** has adapted the structure and constitution of the in- 
habitants of every planet to the nature and circumstances of the 
habitation provided for them, so as to render every portion of 
his dominions a comfortable abode tor his intelligent offspring ; 
provided they do not frustrate his benevolent desigiis (as him

duct. For in no region of the uniTeree, wbatoTer may b» its
